The hook's command gives the interpreter a short starter text that opens the hook program, so that a missing or failing program ends the command with exit code 1 and never with 2. `runtime/launch.py` holds the function that writes a message (`send_message`) and the constant of 800,000 tokens; `run_turn` follows the stream of a running turn and sends the host's message once; the operator's command is `message <instance> <session-id> --file <file>`, and it refuses a session that has no folder for messages. `supervise`, the request to judge an ending again and the compaction window were left as they were. The test that had failed in round 11 gives the review a Claude model itself. A fourth trial on two real sessions used the hook program and the settings as the item wrote them: with the program present a waiting message arrived once, and with it missing the session went on undisturbed (the record of the trials, "A fourth trial"). What section 16.24 builds on this is the next step. The observation on `run_turn` given above was confirmed by three repairers of round 12 and was not repaired; it is handed on with section 16.24.

### 16.18 A sub-agent that sees only the Lean files says what the root states, before the user confirms it

Carried out in round 12, by a passage in "Compile, and have the user confirm" of `reference/operations.md` and one sentence of `SKILL.md`. A decision of the user (26 November, 13:15:02Z: "yeah, sub-agent restate the root before I confirm it"), on a proposal of round 10 that came from the second evaluation (N2, the part its author had marked for the user).

What stands behind it. The operator writes the root's Lean definitions and statement with the user, compiles them with `init`, and has the user read what was fixed; `reference/operations.md` lists what the user reads, under "Compile, and have the user confirm", and tells the operator to explain how the Lean statement reads. That explanation is the explanation of whoever wrote the statement. A misreading of the operator's own passes into it unnoticed, and the whole research rests on what the statement says. Round 10 added a check of the root's statement on small cases; a difference that no small case shows (the order of quantifiers, a range the cases do not reach) remains.

The decision: after the root compiles and before the user confirms, the operator has a sub-agent say in words what the root states, and shows the user where that differs from the root file. The sub-agent is given the Lean files of the canon folder and nothing else: not `root.md`, not the conversation with the user, not the operator's explanation. What it writes is laid beside the statement in words, and each place where the two differ is shown to the user together with the four things the user already reads. The driving session told the user how it reads the decision for an operator whose CLI cannot start a sub-agent, and the user did not object: that operator tells the user that this step was not done.

What moves together, as one item raised by the repairer of the operator's area (1): a passage in "Compile, and have the user confirm" of `reference/operations.md`, and the sentence of `SKILL.md` that points to that section, which names what the user is shown and is brought into agreement if it no longer covers it. The passage is for the operator: it gives the reason (a reading by someone who did not write the statement and was not told what it is meant to say) and what the sub-agent may and may not be given, and leaves the wording of the request to the operator. No program is changed and no check is added: the step is the operator's, as the confirmation itself is.

### 16.19 What each session used is recorded by the program, and a run's total can be read

Carried out in round 12; the last paragraph of this section says where the correction departs from what the section expected. A decision of the user (26 November, 13:15:02Z), on a proposal of round 10 that came from the second evaluation (N5). The driving session had put three options to the user: leave it, a read-only script, or one sentence in the operator's manual. The user chose none of them and said:

> "run's cost should be mechanically stored through what each sessions leave bhind(e.g. leaving used token + token usage types + calculated(or provided) cost in usd)"

So the program itself records, for each session, the tokens it used by kind and the cost in US dollars, from what the session leaves behind. That is the decision. What follows is what is known and the driving session's design; the user was told the three settings marked below and did not object.

**What is known.** A session leaves, for each turn, the stream of its CLI: `cli.stdout.jsonl` in the session's folder for the first turn and under `turns/<number>/` for each resumed turn. The driving session read the retained streams of eighty real Claude sessions that had a resumed turn, without changing anything, and found this.

- The last event of type `result` in a turn's stream carries `usage` for that turn, that is for one call of the CLI: `input_tokens`, `cache_creation_input_tokens`, `cache_read_input_tokens`, `output_tokens` and further keys. These are summed over the turns of a session.
- The same event carries `total_cost_usd`, and that figure is cumulative over the conversation: in all eighty sessions the figure of the resumed turn was at or above that of the first turn, also where the resumed turn had only a few steps. The cost of a session is therefore the figure of its last turn that has one, and not a sum.
- The event also carries `modelUsage`, which gives for each model `inputTokens`, `outputTokens`, `cacheReadInputTokens`, `cacheCreationInputTokens`, `thinkingTokens` and `costUSD`, and further `model_usage_since_last_result`, `subagent_stats`, `num_turns` and `duration_ms`.
- No session of this instance has run on Codex, so what a Codex stream carries is known only from the documentation the investigation fetched (`임시/do-math_검증_20261125-3/조사/E1_scripts/out/docs_codex/`).
- A turn that the CLI did not end by itself (a host that was ended, a machine that went down) has no `result` event.

**The structure to reach.** Three settings are the driving session's and were told to the user.

- *A record in the session's folder, and the totals with the runner.* For each session the program writes a small file into the session's folder that holds, for each turn, the tokens by kind and the cost figure the stream gave, and the session's totals. The runner keeps each collected session's totals in its state, so that `status` can give the total of the run without reading any stream again.
- *The cost is the CLI's own figure.* do-math holds no table of prices, because prices change and a table would go stale. Where a stream gives no cost, the record holds the token counts and leaves the cost empty, and says so; a total over sessions then says how many sessions it could not count.
- *Codex.* The record takes what the stream gives and marks the rest as absent.

The record keeps apart what was measured and what was derived, and what is cumulative and what is for one turn, since a reader who sums a cumulative figure gets a wrong total.

**Where it is written.** The driving session's proposal is the place where the runner collects a session whose host has ended (`Runner.collect` in `runtime/runner.py`), with the reading of a stream in `runtime/retention.py`, which already reads these streams. Three reasons. Every session passes there once, also one whose host died, so no session is left without a record. The runner writes its state there anyway. And `run_turn` of `runtime/ending.py` is rewritten in this round by section 16.17, and `supervise` in the round after it by section 16.24, so a correction that wrote the record from either would share lines with the one or have to be fitted to the other. A session that is still running has no record until it ends; `reference/operations.md` says so where it describes the total. The user's words were first read as a record written when each turn ends; the repairer may reach that later, once section 16.17 has been applied, and does not reach into `runtime/ending.py` for it now.

**What moves together**, as one item raised by the repairer of the runner's area (2): the reading of a turn's last `result` event and the building of a session's record (`runtime/retention.py`); the writing of the record and the keeping of the totals where a session is collected, and the total in what `status` returns (`runtime/runner.py`); tests through the real path, with a stream that has a first and a resumed turn (so that the per-turn sum and the cumulative cost are both asserted), a stream without a `result` event, and a stream without a cost; and `reference/operations.md`, in the table of what `status` prints and where it describes what a session's folder keeps ("Retention and archives"). The sessions of the paper command and of the finalize pipeline are launched by `runtime/command_sessions.py` and not by the runner; the user's answer was given to a question about a research run, and this item leaves those sessions as they are. The repairer writes into its record whether their streams would allow the same.

The file `docs/do-math/running.md`, outside the product of this run, says that do-math collects no usage figures. The driving session corrects it after the last round.

**What round 12 applied, and what it found about the figures.** The record is written where the runner collects a session, as proposed: `retention.usage` reads a session's streams, the runner writes the record into the session's folder and keeps the totals in its state, and `status` gives the total of the run. The figures are read otherwise than this section expected. The repairer read 735 retained streams and found that 69 of them hold several `result` events, each with a `usage` that covers only the span since the previous one, and that `usage` leaves out what delegated sub-agents used. `modelUsage` and `total_cost_usd` are cumulative over the conversation. So the correction takes both the tokens and the cost of a session from the per-model figures of the last turn that reports them, and sums nothing over turns. The statement above that the token counts of a turn's last `result` event are summed over the turns is therefore not what the files do, and the user was told of the correction. Left for later: a record written at the end of each turn, which becomes possible once section 16.24 has been applied; whether a resumed call of Codex reports cumulatively, which no stream on this machine shows; and the sessions of the two commands, whose streams would allow the same reading and which this direction left as they are.

### 16.20 The record of a finished build holds what is read after the build

Carried out in round 12: `settle_build` writes to `result.json` only what is read after the build, and readers take the keys that remain from records of either form. A proposal of round 11, accepted by the three judges and adopted by the driving session: it removes data that nothing reads, changes no decision of the user, and follows what the user asked of the fourth generation, that Lean be used "in a way that consumes resources efficiently" (section 4.1).

What was found. When a build ends, the broker writes its whole record to `builds/<build>/result.json` (`Broker.settle_build` in `runtime/lean_broker.py`). The record has a row for every module the build reached, and a build of a candidate or of a publication reaches every module of the library, which grows with every publication. In the instance on this machine these records came to 3,512 files and 1,058.6 MB on 26 November, and a day's worth had tripled in three days (449 MB on the 26th by 21:46). The largest was 1.7 MB for a build of 960 modules. Of a module's row, the readers of a finished record use `status`, `exit_code`, `reason`, `job`, `key` and `measured`, and of the record itself `status`, `id`, `duplicate_of`, `targets`, `session` and `reason`: `canon.build_outcome`, `lean_broker.report_build`, `check.audit_formal`, `await_record` and `cancel`. The keys `source`, `source_sha256`, `olean`, `imports`, `local`, `needs`, `reads`, `public` and `full` of a row, and `sources` of the record, are read only while the build runs, from the broker's state. With those left out, the records of the last twenty-four hours came to 12 percent of their size.

The direction: the record written at the end of a build holds what is read after the build and no more. The record in the broker's state while the build runs is unchanged. A reader of finished records reads both forms, since records of the old form stay on disk and a broker that has not been restarted goes on writing them. Raised by the repairer of Lean and the broker (7) as one item: `settle_build`, any test that reads from a finished record a key that is no longer written (the repairer of round 11 saw `olean` read in two tests of `runtime/tests/test_lean_broker.py` and did not establish whether from a finished record), and the sentence of `reference/lean-broker.md` on what `result.json` keeps. `reference/lean.md` tells a session that a module's entry has `status`, `exit_code` and `job`; those stay.

What was not adopted, and is not raised: a rule by which the broker deletes the folders of finished builds and jobs. It would bind the volume more tightly, but it needs a decision on when a record may go, and the user has been told that this is theirs to ask for.

### 16.21 A review job that has closed no longer carries the hashes of its candidate

Carried out in round 12: the three places at which a review job closes call one helper that takes the hashes out of its task. The hashes of jobs that had closed before stay in the state. A proposal of round 11, accepted by the three judges and adopted by the driving session for the same reasons as section 16.20.

What was found. `Runner.finish` puts into the task of a review job a hash for every file of the candidate (`candidate_hashes`). The places that read them are `recover_candidate` and `advance_build` in `runtime/runner.py` and the check of a review's output in `runtime/check.py`, which reads the session's `input.json`; all three concern a job that is ending or being published. A job is closed at three places (a review that decides against publication, the end of a publication, and the replacement of a peer), and none of them removes the hashes. In the instance on this machine the hashes of 127 closed review jobs were 1.69 MB of a state file of 4.0 MB, and the runner writes that file whole at every step while sessions' commands, the broker and `status` read it whole.

The direction: where a review job closes, the hashes leave its task. A job that is tried again keeps them. The task of a living job and the session's `input.json` are unchanged. Raised by the repairer of the runner's area (2) as an item of its own, apart from the item of section 16.19: the three places in `runtime/runner.py`, and the tests that assert the hashes of a job (the repairer of round 11 named `runtime/tests/test_publication.py` and `runtime/tests/test_resilience.py` and did not establish whether any of them looks at a closed job).

### 16.22 A peer whose turn of judging its ending again fails keeps the candidate its first turn prepared

Carried out in round 12. `Runner.finish` tells the case apart by three things the host leaves (a resumption was made, the host wrote its exit, and no cancellation was asked for) and not by the exit code, because the host records a refused turn as interrupted also when the CLI ended with exit code 0. A decision of the user (26 November, 13:37:10Z: "for both, go with your recommendation"), on a proposal of round 11. The recommendation was the third of four options the driving session had put to the user.

What stands behind it. When a session's first turn ends normally, the host resumes the same conversation so that the session judges its ending again. When that resumed turn ends in a refusal of the provider, the host authorizes the end as `interrupted`, and `Runner.finish` in `runtime/runner.py` then treats the peer as an interrupted stage: the job goes back to `pending`, and a new peer receives the folder of the old one as partial work. The candidate that the first turn prepared reaches neither the check of the session's output nor the freezing of the candidate. In the instance on this machine this happened to two of 137 peers, both on 24 November, both through a refusal that the provider classified as extraction of reasoning, one of them right after a compaction; the peers that replaced them ran for 191 and 373 minutes. A session that judges its ending again has, as a rule, finished its work: every one of the 130 peers of that instance that ended had decided its ending after one resumption. The program already has an end without interruption for a session that could not be asked again: when the conversation cannot be resumed, the host authorizes the end as `resume_unavailable`, and a peer's output then goes to the check of its output and on to review.

The decision. A peer whose turn of judging its ending again ends with an exit code other than 0 is not treated as interrupted, unless the operator cancelled it. `Runner.finish` treats such a session as ended, as it treats one whose conversation could not be resumed, whatever the host recorded as the ground; if its `output/` passes the check of a session's output, the candidate is frozen and goes to review, and if it does not, a replacement peer takes the stage. That last clause describes what happens today and decides nothing about it. A failure of the first turn, a cancellation by `kill-session`, and what a refusal leaves behind for the model's availability stay as they are. The user was told the cost: such a session passes on without having judged its ending a second time, as already happens at the limit of resumptions and when a conversation cannot be resumed. What guards the candidate is the check of the session's output, which refuses a candidate that was changed after it was prepared, and review, which judges it.

What moves together, as one item raised by the repairer of the area "the life of one session" (5): the lines of `Runner.finish` that decide between an interrupted stage and a normal end; a test of the path through the runner (a peer whose first turn ended with exit code 0 and whose resumed turn failed: with a prepared candidate it goes to review, with a candidate changed after preparation it is replaced); and the sentences of `reference/operations.md` that describe the present behaviour, under "Session hosts and continuation turns" and "Refusals, failed outputs and damaged candidates". The correction is written in `runtime/runner.py` and does not change `runtime/ending.py`: `run_turn` is rewritten in this round by section 16.17, and `supervise` in the round after it by section 16.24. If the repairer finds that the case cannot be told apart in `Runner.finish` from what the host has recorded, it says so in its record and raises what stands.

### 16.23 A finding that establishes the confirmation of a proof may say whether the proof is accepted

Carried out in round 12: a finding that names an element in `validates` may carry `accepted` for it. The sentence of `roles/attempt.md` on a proof that was held back was left as it is, and the item gave its reason. A decision of the user (26 November, 13:37:10Z, the same message), on a proposal of round 11. The recommendation was the second of three options, without the narrowing its author had proposed.

What stands behind it. The standing of a proof comes from two judgments of review: whether the proof is correct, and whether a correct proof is accepted (section 4.5 gives the two considerations of acceptance and what the user decided on them). A review of a candidate writes both for each element. A later review, a comprehensive one or a review whose assessment carries findings, can establish or restore the confirmation of a proof that is already published, by naming the element in `validates`. (Section 2 introduces a finding by its commonest use, the withdrawal of a confirmation; the form of a finding serves both.) In this section a finding that establishes a confirmation is one that names the element in `validates`, whether the confirmation is new or restored. The form of a finding has no place for acceptance, and `knowledge.apply_findings` writes `correct` and leaves `accepted` as it was. A proof that the review of its candidate left unconfirmed or undetermined was not accepted then, since an unconfirmed proof cannot be. When a later review judges that proof correct, it stands as "correct, held back", although no review ever asked the two considerations of it; the reason shown with it says that the proof is correct and not what could be made simpler; `reference/operations.md` tells the operator that such a standing means review held the proof back for one of the two reasons; and the statement stays among the work that remains until an attempt, a peer and a review have passed the same proof through again, which no guide tells them to do. This was followed in the program and the guides; it has not been seen in an instance.

The decision. A finding that establishes the confirmation of a proof may also say whether that proof is accepted. The review that confirms has read the proof to the point of judging it correct, so it can ask the two considerations of it as the review of a candidate does. The author of the proposal had recommended this for the findings of a comprehensive review only; the driving session recommended it for any finding that establishes a confirmation, so that one rule holds, and that is what the user chose.

What moves together, as one item raised by the repairer of the area of the checks and of the review and organizer roles (4): the form of a finding and its check (`check.findings_check` in `runtime/check.py`); the application of a finding to an element's verdict (`knowledge.apply_findings` in `runtime/knowledge.py`); the table of what a finding changes in `reference/review-output.md`, and the passages of `roles/review.md` on a comprehensive review and on findings, which tell a review that it judges acceptance where it establishes a confirmation; the sentence of `reference/operations.md` on what "correct, held back" means, and the sentence of `roles/attempt.md` on taking on a proof that was held back, as far as they still say something untrue; and tests through the real path. Three things the item settles from the files and states in its 영향 칸. First, a finding without the new key behaves as now: a proof that had been accepted before its confirmation was withdrawn is accepted again, and one that never was stays held back. A finding written by a session that was launched before the change therefore gives today's result. The present `findings_check` reads the keys it knows and passes over any other, so a runner that has not been restarted takes a finding with the new key and does nothing with it; the repairer confirms this in the copy. Second, an acceptance is taken only for an element that the same finding names in `validates`. Third, what a review writes when the instance has switched off one or both considerations of acceptance agrees with what the review of a candidate writes in that instance. The key is an addition to a form that sessions write; the user decided it, so it is not raised as a proposal (freeze document, 영역 배분, 「아직 수행되지 않은 방향」).

### 16.24 A session whose context is nearly full hands its task over to a new session of its role

Decisions of the user of 26 November, made in one exchange after round 11 (13:46Z to 14:15Z) and approved as a whole ("approved", 14:14:57Z). Carried out in round 13, on the message system of section 16.17, which round 12 had applied; the last two paragraphs of this section say what the correction did and what is left.

**What the user said.** The user proposed (13:46:29Z):

> "about that 800k wrap-up message. what about, rather than having that be the end, after the session who writes the wrap-up output finishes, we dispatch a new session of same role with the same pack + the wrap-up output as "continuation of" that previous session? that would be repeated until the session reports its task as done. we may just also disable autocompact and raise the wrap-up to 900k maybe."

and gave its purpose (13:51:12Z):

> "I want it to write an output of its research, most of which is thinking, in a way that is more predictable and better than what autocompact does, so that a new session can continue from that point on"

The driving session then put the decisions to the user one at a time. The user's answers are the decisions:

1. *A chain of sessions is one stage* ("yes, A", 13:53:37Z). Nothing is published at a hand-over. The next session takes over the same task, with the same sections, leases and question, and the work goes to a peer and to review only when a link reports the task done. A chain is one long session in which a written hand-over stands where a compaction stood.
2. *Only a link's own judgement ends a chain* ("no, let the session judge", 13:55:53Z). The user was told that a filling context has so far limited how much one session spends on one task, and that a chain on a target that does not yield can run for days while it holds its sections and a place among the sessions. The runner counts no links and sets no limit.
3. *The hand-over is the report the guides already ask for.* The driving session had proposed a new standard of eight parts; the user asked "rather than recreating something new, can we simply reuse the output format and the directives that we already have?" (13:58:00Z), and the comparison showed that the table of `reference/retained-work.md`, "The report", already asks for nearly all of it. The user decided: "yes, with those two additions" (13:59:42Z), and on how they are added, "yes, add them in the table and the two guides" (14:02:42Z). The two additions:
   - Two clauses in that table and no new row. "Current work" ends: "…the current approach and what is being investigated now, with the argument in hand as far as it goes and the point at which it is still open." "Remaining work and artifacts" begins: "What you would do next and why, unresolved opportunities, remaining obligations, …". The words added are "with the argument in hand as far as it goes and the point at which it is still open" and "What you would do next and why,"; the user approved these words, and a correction keeps them. The clauses hold for every report, also one that a peer takes over.
   - A peer has no report; its `output/` is the candidate. A peer that hands its task over writes the same sections, as far as they apply to a candidate under construction, into `work/report.md`: what of the candidate stands in `output/`, what is still to be written or compiled, and what it decided and why. One sentence says so in `reference/retained-work.md`, where that guide says that the report belongs to the producing session. One sentence in `roles/peer.md`, beside the passage on taking the place of an earlier peer, tells a peer that continues a hand-over to start from the earlier peer's `work/report.md` and candidate; the passage beside it tells a replacement to form its own understanding first, which is meant for a peer that failed.
4. *Automatic compaction is switched off for worker sessions, and the message is sent at 800,000 tokens* ("compaction off and 800k", 14:06:47Z). This replaces the compaction window of 950,000 tokens. The user was shown what room a session has after the message at 800,000 and at 900,000, given that the message arrives one step late (section 22 has the sizes of that step).
5. *What peers publish and what review accepts stay as they are.* The user had passed on an outside assessment of the running research: volume grows away from the frontier while the frontier's top does not move, and its author infers that sessions produce what can be proved nearby and that review accepts whatever is correct. Asked whether the cause should be investigated in real sessions first, the user decided: "rather than investigating, let's see if this new setup improves things" (14:12:43Z). The user has since given a rule on how a session chooses its work, which is section 16.27; it changes guides of the producing roles and nothing in this direction.

**What the driving session settled, and the user approved with the whole.** These five are therefore decided, and a correction does not reverse them. Where the files make one of them impossible as written, the repairer takes the nearest course that keeps its purpose and says so in the item.

- *Who it covers.* Every role the runner launches on Claude. A Codex worker receives no message (section 16.17) and keeps its present compaction setting. The user's decision on a session without a report names the peer. A review and an organizer have neither a report nor, today, an end decision; they have not reached a compaction in the instance on this machine. For them the driving session sets the same as for a peer, and this setting is its own: such a session writes the sections of the report, as far as they apply to its work, into `work/report.md`, and marks the hand-over by the end decision on the third ground, the one occasion on which it writes an end decision. The repairer may settle it otherwise from the files.
- *What a continuation receives.* A session folder of its own; the pack of its role as the runner builds it at that moment, with the same assignment and the canon as it then is; and, in its task, the folders of the earlier links. The user's words were "the same pack + the wrap-up output"; the driving session reads "the same pack" as the pack of the same role and assignment and told the user so. Sections, leases and the question stay with the job. The earlier links' conversation records are there to look something up in, and are not meant to be read through.
- *How a hand-over is marked.* By the end decision the guides already describe (`reference/retained-work.md`, "Continuing an attempt, exploration, formalization or peer session"). The message at 800,000 tokens is itself a request to decide the ending and carries a request ID; the session answers it in the same turn with `output/end-decision.json`. If its task is done, it answers as now, on one of the two grounds the guides define (`task_completed`, or `no_way_forward` under the standard the guides set for it). If the task is to go on, it answers with a third ground. The message is a request in the sense of the freeze document's invariant, so any of the three answers is the answer to a request and the host authorizes the end on it without a further turn: a conversation that is nearly full is not opened once more, and handing a task over is no ending of research (freeze document, 불변식 「연구를 계속하게 하는 장치를 약화하지 않는다」). On the third ground the runner launches the continuation. A session that ends a turn without having been sent the message, which is the ordinary ending of a session and of the last link of a chain, is resumed to judge its ending again exactly as today.
- *One request at a time.* Two cases follow from how a message is delivered, and the repairer settles each so that a decision is valid only for the request that asked for it. A session can pass 800,000 tokens and end its turn before a tool call hands the message over; the request the host opened is then unanswered, and the host resumes the conversation once with that request as its prompt, as it resumes for any request. And a message can fall into a resumed turn that already answers a request; there is then still one request, and the session is told that the third ground is among its answers.
- *When the limit is reached all the same.* With compaction off the limit is hard, and a trial on a real session showed what the host then sees (below). The runner starts the continuation from the files as they stand and does not count the failure against the model. What is lost is what that link had not yet written.
- *The guide's passage on a compaction.* The section of `reference/retained-work.md` on what a compaction leaves, and the sentence of `resume/common.md`, describe what no longer happens to a session that hands over. The section becomes the passage on the hand-over. What it says of a compaction stays true for a session whose conversation is still replaced by a summary (a Codex worker, and a session that receives no message), so those facts remain in it, said for that case and in as few words as keep every detail; a session recognises the case by the summary that then stands in its conversation, and need not know which client runs it.

**What the trials established.** Their record is `임시/do-math_검증_20261125-3/조사/E2_메시지_훅_시험.md`.

- *The limit with compaction off* ("A third trial"). A real session launched with `DISABLE_AUTO_COMPACT=1` and held to a 200K window read files until its next request would have exceeded the window. That request was refused: the turn ended with exit code 1, the stream's last `assistant` event carried `"error": "invalid_request"` with the text "Prompt is too long", and the `result` event carried `"is_error": true`, `"terminal_reason": "blocking_limit"` and the same text. Nothing was summarised. The same session with compaction left on compacted twice and did not fail. A host can therefore tell this ending from a provider's refusal by the `result` event. The trial used a small model at a 200K window; a worker's own model at one million tokens was not tried, nor whether such a conversation can be resumed.
- *The hook command of round 12* ("A fourth trial"). With the hook program present, a waiting message reached a real session once and was moved to `messages/delivered/`; with it missing, the command ended with exit code 1 and the session went on undisturbed.
- *Not run.* A trial of how a real session with a worker's own model takes the wording of the message was planned and was not run. How a session takes it is seen first on real research sessions, after a runner has been started with this direction. The design point stands: in five retained streams of the running instance the provider's safeguard stopped a response, twice classified as extraction of reasoning, so the message asks for the record of the research in the form of the report and not for the session's reasoning.

**What is in the files now** (round 12; the repairer confirms each in the copy).

- `runtime/launch.py`: `CONTEXT_WINDOW`, `AUTO_COMPACT_TOKENS` (950,000) and `CONTEXT_MESSAGE_TOKENS` (800,000); `worker_environment`, which sets the compaction window for a Claude worker; `worker_command`, whose settings hold the hook for a session that receives messages; `send_message`, `receives_messages` and `open_messages`.
- `runtime/ending.py`: `CONTEXT_MESSAGE`, the text the host sends, which today names the compaction window and asks the session to bring its files up to date and end its turn; `run_turn`, which follows the stream and sends that text once; `end_decision`, which accepts a decision only as the answer to a reconsideration request and only with the grounds `task_completed` and `no_way_forward`; and `supervise`, in which the first turn has no request, so that a decision written during it is set aside and the session is resumed to judge its ending. For a review and an organizer `supervise` asks for no end decision: such a session is done when its output passes the check of a session's output.
- `runtime/runner.py`: `Runner.finish`, which treats an authorization on the ground `interrupted` as an unfinished stage, and `Runner.retry`, which puts the job back and enters the old session's folder in the task's `previous_partial_sessions`; `Runner.replace_peer`, which gives a new peer the folders of the earlier peers; `Runner.dispatch`, which creates the message folders.
- The texts under `resume/`: `common.md` is the frame of the request to judge an ending, with the request ID and the two grounds, and `output.md` the frame for a review and an organizer.

**What moves together**, raised by the repairer of standpoint 19. The program, its tests and the passages of the guides that describe what the program does are consistent only together and are one item. A part that leaves the files consistent when it is applied alone may be an item of its own, so that one lost detail does not undo the whole; the two clauses of the report table are such a part. If the whole cannot be written out in one round, the repairer raises what stands by itself and writes in its record what is left; it raises no proposal for the remainder.

- *The message as a request* (`runtime/ending.py`, `runtime/launch.py`). When the host sends the message it opens a request with an ID, and the text carries that ID and says what the three answers are. The text no longer speaks of a compaction to come for a session whose compaction is off. `supervise` takes the decision of the turn in which the message was sent as the answer to that request.
- *The third ground* (`end_decision`, the format of `output/end-decision.json` in `reference/retained-work.md`, the frames under `resume/` where they name the grounds). A decision on that ground is authorized without a further turn. For a review and an organizer, which write no end decision today, the repairer settles from the files how such a session marks a hand-over, and says so in the item.
- *The continuation* (`runtime/runner.py`, `runtime/context.py`). On that authorization `Runner.finish` does not pass the work on to a peer or to review: it launches a new session of the same role on the same job, with the earlier links' folders in its task, in the way `retry` and `replace_peer` already hand a folder on. It is no failure: no block is placed on a model, no attempt of the job is used up, and the pipeline keeps its sections, leases and question. The pack tells the continuing session where the earlier link's report is.
- *The limit reached all the same.* A turn that ended with `terminal_reason` `blocking_limit` is told apart from a provider's refusal (`runtime/retention.py`, `refusal`) and from a failed launch, and the runner starts the continuation from the files without a block on the model.
- *Compaction off* (`worker_environment` and the constants of `runtime/launch.py`; the passage of `reference/operations.md` under "Context, continuation and acceptance settings"; the assertions of `runtime/tests/test_context.py`). `DISABLE_AUTO_COMPACT=1` for a Claude worker that receives messages, the Codex setting as it is. A session that cannot be sent the message keeps its compaction window, because for it a compaction is the only way past the limit: a session of the paper command or of the finalize pipeline, which `launch.worker_command` also serves, and a session that a runner started before the correction has launched. This is the driving session's setting. It also settles what happens between an old and a new program: a host is started from the files as they then stand, while a runner that is already running keeps the program it loaded. Such a runner makes no folder for messages, so its sessions are sent no message, keep their compaction, and never end on a ground that runner does not know.
- *The guides.* `reference/retained-work.md`: the two clauses of the table, the sentence for a peer that hands over, a short passage on the hand-over itself (when it happens, what the next session is given, how a session marks that its task goes on), the format of the end decision, and the section on a compaction as far as it no longer holds. `roles/peer.md`: the one sentence for the peer that receives. `resume/common.md`: the sentence on a compaction. `reference/tools.md`, where round 12 described messages, if it names what the message asks. `reference/operations.md`: what a worker is launched with, how the operator tells from what `status` and the session folders hold that a session continues another, and the sentences on the reconsideration turn that no longer hold for a hand-over. `status` gains output only if the operator could not otherwise tell, and the item then says so.
- *Tests through the real path:* a session that answers the message with the third ground and is continued by a new session of its role that receives the earlier folder; a session that answers it with `task_completed`; a turn that ends at the limit; and the environment of a Claude and of a Codex worker.

Three observations of repairers of round 12 lie in lines this item changes, and are handed on with it (the repairer's file under `임시/do-math_검증_20261125-3/평가/` has them): the `try` of `run_turn` covers the whole supervision of the turn, so that an `OSError` after the CLI has started is recorded as a failed launch and the runner blocks the model; no test asserts that a resumed turn rewrites the session's `process.json`; and `Runner.collect` takes every key of the host's `exit.json` into the session's state, so a key added to what `run_turn` returns enters the runner's state file. The first is a defect in lines the item rewrites. The repairer establishes it in the copy and carries its repair in the item, saying so in the 영향 칸, as the freeze document allows for a second defect in a passage that is rewritten; it is raised neither as an item of its own, which would cross this one, nor as a proposal. What the repair has to decide is how a failure of the host's own writing ends once the CLI runs; the driving session's suggestion is that it is recorded as a failure of the host and not of the launch, so that no block falls on the model. If the repairer cannot settle it from the files, it leaves that behaviour as it is and says so in its record.

**What is not changed.** The threshold of 800,000 tokens and that compaction is off are the user's. No limit is set on the number of links. What a peer publishes and what review accepts stay as they are. The coordination messages keep their delivery. Section 16.27 changes the passages on choosing work and on holding on to a problem in `roles/attempt.md`, `roles/explore.md`, `roles/formalizer.md` and `roles/peer.md`, `resume/attempt.md`, the section "Working directly on the central difficulty" of `reference/common.md`, one passage of `reference/sections.md`, and the sentence of `reference/retained-work.md` under "Reporting that all the work that remains is occupied"; this item leaves those passages alone, so that the two items share no line. Section 16.26 has "Retention and archives" of `reference/operations.md` and the sentence of `roles/peer.md` on the raw form of a producer's conversation.

**How the user will tell whether it helped.** After a runner has been restarted with it: whether chains form and the reports are written to their standard, whether a session still reaches the limit, and whether new volume lands nearer the frontier than before (on 24 to 26 November the library of the running instance grew by 30,000 to 37,000 lines a day, while the frontier showed 101 lines, 19 of them without a proof, under an open conjecture). The driving session reports the figures; whether they are an improvement is the user's judgement.

A session that is already running keeps the settings its host wrote when it was launched. Sessions are launched with the new setting only by a runner started after the correction.

**What round 13 applied.** The repairer of standpoint 19 wrote the direction as one item of forty-nine pieces, with the two clauses of the report table as an item of their own, and the three judges accepted both. The host's message at 800,000 tokens (`CONTEXT_MESSAGE` in `runtime/ending.py`) is a request to decide the ending and carries the ID of that request. A session whose work is to go on brings its report up to date and answers with the basis `handed_over` in `output/end-decision.json`; a peer, a review and an organizer write the sections of the report to `work/report.md`. `end_decision` accepts that basis only as the answer to that request, and `supervise` authorizes the end on it without a further turn. A turn that ended at the limit of the window (`retention.context_limit`, which reads `terminal_reason` `blocking_limit` from the turn's `result` event) is authorized by the host on the basis `context_limit`, and only for a session that has a folder for messages. On either basis `Runner.finish` calls `Runner.hand_over`: the job is put back for a new session of the same role, no block is placed on a model and no attempt of the job is used up, the sections or the question stay held, and the task lists the folders of the earlier sessions, oldest first, under `continues`. `runtime/context.py` tells the continuing session in its pack where the last report is. `launch.worker_environment` sets `DISABLE_AUTO_COMPACT=1` for a Claude worker that receives messages; every other session keeps the compaction window. `reference/retained-work.md` has the section "Handing a task over to a new session" where the section on a compaction stood, and keeps the facts of a compaction for a session whose conversation is still replaced by a summary. The observation on `run_turn` was repaired in the item: its `try` covers the start of the CLI only, so an error of the host after the CLI runs is no longer recorded as a failed launch. `status` gained no output. Where the correction departs from this section it says so in the item, and the judges weighed each departure.

**What is left.** By the repairer's own record, no test takes a peer or an organizer through a hand-over as far as the runner; the test covers an attempt, an explorer, and a review whose turn ended at the limit. Two judges noted that one sentence of the item's account is wrong for a formalizer: when the declaration is carried over, its section is written again under the new session's ID (`Runner.dispatch`), so that the runner's evaluation of what is available can run once more; they found nothing broken by it. Other repairers of round 13 recorded two things about a chain that they did not raise: a review that receives the work of a chain is pointed at one producing session and is told nothing of the earlier links' folders, and a session that handed its task over has its waiting compiles cancelled, so the session that continues builds again, with the modules already compiled coming from the cache. These four are handed to the repairer of standpoint 19 (its file under `임시/do-math_검증_20261125-3/평가/`). Three behaviours of the CLI on which the correction rests have not been seen on a real session: the limit at a window of one million tokens with a worker's own model, how a session takes the message, and the delivery of a message in a resumed turn.

### 16.25 A concern is shown against the proof it was raised about, and not against a proof that came in later

Carried out in round 13; the last paragraph of this section says how. A decision of the user (27 November: "1c"), on a proposal of round 12. The driving session had put three options to the user; the third was the recommendation.

What stands behind it. A concern is a reported challenge to the proof of an element (`reference/common.md`), and the program attaches it to the element's name: `add_concerns` in `runtime/knowledge.py` records the names of the elements it targets and the ID of the publication that carried it, and nothing that says which proof it was raised about. A comprehensive review can confirm a concern and withdraw the confirmation of the proof. If the statement is on the frontier, an attempt then takes it on, a peer's candidate replaces the proof, and the review of that candidate confirms and accepts the new proof; `change_event` writes the new verdict and leaves the concern as it is. From then on every replay attaches the concern to the element (`settle`), and `frontier_notes` lists it because its state is not `cleared`. Every session launched afterwards receives the line in its pack, with the frontier view, and the view of the element shows an accepted proof and under it a confirmed concern. The guide tells a session that would rely on an element under a concern to establish the fact separately, avoid the questioned part, continue conditionally or choose another route, so sessions treat a confirmed and accepted proof as doubted. Nothing clears the state: only a finding that names the concern changes it, and the runner assigns a concern to a comprehensive review once. A second case has the same root: when an organizer's retirement moves a proof under another name, `organization_event` moves the verdict and leaves the concern's target on the name that no longer exists. This was followed in the program and the guides; in the instance on this machine the one concern there is stands on a proof that has not been replaced.

The decision. The program records when a concern was raised, by the sequence of the publication that carried it, and no longer shows the concern against a proof that came in after it. Where the proof an element has now was brought in by a later change (the verdict carries that change's sequence), the concern leaves the list of the frontier view, and the view of the element marks it as raised about an earlier proof. At a retirement the concern's target moves with the verdict. The program decides by the order of publications only and judges no mathematics. What is given up was told to the user: if the new proof has the same flaw, the old concern no longer points at it; that proof was judged by the review of the candidate that brought it in.

What moves together, as one item raised by the repairer of the area of the canon, the knowledge model and publication (3): in `runtime/knowledge.py`, `add_concerns`, the concern that `apply_findings` opens when a confirmation is withdrawn, `organization_event`, `settle` and `frontier_notes`; the element view in `runtime/canon_tool.py`; tests through the real path (a concern, its confirmation, a replacement of the proof, and what the frontier view and the element view then show; a retirement with a concern on the proof that moves); and the sentences that describe a concern, in `reference/common.md`, `reference/canon.md`, `reference/review-output.md` and `reference/operations.md`, as far as they say what a session or the operator sees. Three things the item settles from the files and states in its 영향 칸. First, what `commands.updates` gives a session for such a concern, so that the views and the updates do not disagree. Second, what the keys and the form of `knowledge/state.json` and of the frontier view become; a key that is added is an addition the user decided, and a session's command and a runner that has not been restarted both read the state. Third, what a concern raised before this correction shows, in an instance whose events carry no such sequence for it.

**What round 13 applied.** The correction does not record a sequence on a concern and compare two numbers, as this section expected: at a retirement that comparison gives a wrong answer, because the verdict that moves keeps the sequence of the change that first brought the proof. Instead, at the event that changes a proof (`proof_changed` in `runtime/knowledge.py`, which `change_event` and `organization_event` call), every concern that stands on the element at that moment has the element's name entered under its key `earlier`; at a retirement the concern's target moves with the verdict. `frontier_notes` leaves out a target that is under `earlier`, and the element view of `runtime/canon_tool.py` shows such a concern with the words "raised about an earlier proof". `settle` was left as it was. A finding published before the proof changed is not marked by this correction; section 16.30 is about that.

### 16.26 The archiver packs a finished session's raw conversation with its work folder

Carried out in round 13; the last paragraph of this section says how. A decision of the user (27 November: "3b"), on a proposal of round 12. The second of four options, which was the recommendation.

What stands behind it. When a session ends, its conversation is kept in its folder in three forms, none compressed: the stream of each turn (`cli.stdout.jsonl`, and the same under `turns/`), the copy of the CLI's own transcript (`transcript-<id>.jsonl`) with the folder beside it (`transcript-<id>/`), and `conversation.md`, the readable text drawn from the transcript (`capture` in `runtime/retention.py`). The archiver (`archive_work` in `runtime/runtime_support.py`, called through `archive_finished` in `runtime/runner.py`) packs only `work/` into an xz archive, for a session whose pipeline has ended and which holds no place among the sessions. In the instance on this machine the session folders came to 9.5 GB for 458 sessions after four days. In a sample of 58 folders the two raw forms, stream and copied transcript, were 62 percent, and the two of five sessions compressed to an eighth with the archiver's preset. After a session has been collected no program reads either raw form; `capture` runs once for each session. Their readers are people and sessions: a peer reads its producer's `conversation.md` and is told that the raw form remains available, a designated review may look at the originals of ended sessions through `retention.json`, and the operator is told where a sub-agent's conversation is.

The decision. When the archiver packs a finished session's `work/`, it also puts that session's two raw forms, the streams of its turns and the file `transcript-<id>.jsonl`, into an xz archive and removes them from their place. Whether that is the archive of `work/` or one of its own is the repairer's to settle; a session that already has `work.tar.xz` needs a second file in any case. `conversation.md`, `retention.json`, the folder `transcript-<id>/` that `conversation.md` points into, `output/`, `context/` and `pack.md` stay where they are. No original is lost or altered, which is what the user decided for a session's own account (section 4.1: retained outside the canon, unchanged); the archiver's comparison of hashes before it removes anything is kept.

What moves together, as one item raised by the repairer of the area "the life of one session" (5): what the archiver packs (`archive_work` and the function beside it that lists the entries, `Runner.archive` and `archive_finished`); how a session's folder says that its raw forms are in an archive, since `retention.json` names them and `roles/review.md` sends a review along that file (the file stays in its place; whether its content changes is part of what the item settles); the passages that tell a reader where the raw forms are (`reference/operations.md`, "Retention and archives", and `roles/review.md` where it speaks of the originals of ended sessions); the sentence of `roles/peer.md` that the raw stream or copied transcript remains available, which the repairer confirms stays true for a peer, whose producer's sessions are not yet taken by the archiver, and changes only if it does not; and the tests of the archiver in `runtime/tests/test_retention.py` and `runtime/tests/test_research.py`. Four things the item settles from the files and states. First, a session that already has `work.tar.xz`: the archiver does not overwrite an archive, and every finished session of a running instance is in that state. Second, that the record of what a session used (section 16.19) is written when the session is collected, before anything is packed. Third, that which sessions the archiver takes is unchanged: the earlier links of a chain (section 16.24) belong to a pipeline that has not ended and are not touched. Fourth, what a session that was launched before the correction and is told that the raw form is in place finds. Removing a file the program wrote is what the user decided here, so it is not raised as a proposal (freeze document, 영역 배분, 「아직 수행되지 않은 방향」). Section 16.24 leaves "Retention and archives" and that sentence of `roles/peer.md` to this item.

**What round 13 applied.** The archiver writes a second archive, `raw.tar.xz`, beside `work.tar.xz` (`archive_raw` in `runtime/runtime_support.py`; `archive_finished` calls it apart from the archive of `work/`, so a session that already has `work.tar.xz` still gets its raw forms packed). `retention.json` is unchanged. `reference/operations.md`, under "Retention and archives", and `roles/review.md` say where the raw forms are; the sentence of `roles/peer.md` was left as it was, since a peer's producer has not been taken by the archiver. The archiver runs as a process of its own at each pass and reads the files as they then stand, so in an instance whose runner has not been restarted the first pass after the correction packs the raw forms of every finished session.

### 16.27 A session chooses the work that matters most to the research, and does not turn from it because it is hard

An instruction of the user, carried out in round 13; the last paragraph of this section says how, and section 16.29 has what the driving session settled of the matters this section left open. The user gave the running instance a rule as its third operator input, approved its wording, and then had it sent to this run with the words "what about simply sending the user input to the verification run and have it apply it to the directives?" (26 November, 15:54:05Z, in the session that operates the instance). The user confirmed it to the driving session on 27 November. The input stands in the instance as `.skill-workspace/sunflower-20261122/control/inputs/1795708300802162100-4606570265a64b6db45b3ebbdfb39225.txt`. Its text, without the three sentences that belong to the input and to no guide (that the user approved the wording, what "your guides already carry", and the sentence on operator inputs 1 and 2):

> # Operator input 3: choosing work that matters when it is hard
>
> This input gives a rule from the user of this research instance. It concerns how you choose what to work on.
>
> ## What the user has seen
>
> A session reads the frontier and finds that the statements on it are difficult. It thinks of ways in, checks them against the library, and finds them already recorded. It then gives its time to something it can finish, in a part of the research where a result changes little. The user calls this shying away from what is hard, and draws a line: "It is one thing to recognize difficulty. It is another to be afraid of it, to avoid it." The user asks sessions for more courage in this sense: to recognize difficulty and not to avoid it.
>
> ## The rule
>
> Work on what matters most to the research, and do not turn away from it because it is hard. How hard a piece of work is tells you what to expect of it and how to go about it. It is not, by itself, a reason to choose the work or to leave it.
>
> The choice of work is yours, and you make it by weighing what is open to you. The chance of success, the gain if the work succeeds and the insight it would give are among the things to weigh. Weigh them together: a small chance at something the research needs can be worth more than a sure result it needs less. Do not choose a piece of work because it is the hardest, or because it is the easiest. Choose the work that would do most for the research, the work it most needs in order to progress, however daunting that work is. When you see work beyond the frontier that would do more than what the frontier shows, you can choose it, and when nothing the frontier shows seems to you to be what the research most needs, you can look for such work.
>
> ## How to tell what matters
>
> There is no simple rule for what matters, and you should not look for a set of rules to hand this judgment to. The research itself usually shows it: in an obstacle at which several approaches have stopped, in something that would give great insight into the open issues, in something that could connect several separate things into a larger pattern, perhaps a general one, and in other ways. Insight and general patterns of this kind count in their own right, as they relate to settling the root; they do not have to be a step in a proof of it. Judge from what you see in the research.
>
> ## When what matters is an obstacle
>
> Often the work that matters most is an obstacle that no one yet knows how to pass. In front of such an obstacle, other work can look better because it offers something you can do and complete. The user values these differently. Effort spent where it matters, even when it ends without a result, is worth more than a result produced where it matters less.
>
> So when an obstacle is important, try to overcome it. Try when you are not sure of succeeding, and try when you think you will probably fail. Trying does not mean repeating an approach just as it failed before. It means staying with the obstacle and thinking hard about how to get past it where the earlier approaches could not.
>
> ## The standing of this rule
>
> This is a soft rule. You should follow it, and it is more than advice. It is not enforced. Review does not apply it: it is no ground for judging a proof or for holding one back, and it is not part of review's account of what a change contributes. No check of the runner applies it.

**What is the user's.** The rule as that text states it, and the following, which the user decided while the text was written. They bind a correction as the text does.

- It is a soft rule: a session should follow it, it is more than advice, and nothing enforces it. No check is added for it.
- Review does not apply it, in judging a proof or in its account of what a change contributes. The guides of review are not changed by this direction, and no passage is written that would lead a review to apply it.
- A session chooses by its own weighing. It does not choose the hardest work because it is the hardest, nor the easiest because it is the easiest.
- Effort where it matters, even without a result, is worth more than a result where it matters less. A session tries an important obstacle even when it expects to fail, which does not mean repeating an approach as it failed.
- No rule decides what matters. A session judges it from the research; the signs the text names are examples and the list is open.
- On work beyond the frontier the user said: "I want sessions to be able to choose out-of-frontier options when it sees it, or seek them out when they see no satisfying alternative, but not treat it on a same level as the frontier", and of the wording: "What I want you to do is reduce the emphasis, not add an explicit counter emphasis." The text carries this in one sentence that gives the two conditions, placed once, at the end of a paragraph. Nothing states a priority for the frontier. A guide keeps to that: the two conditions, said once, without a sentence that sets the frontier above or below.
- Insight and general patterns count in their own right, as they relate to settling the root; they need not be a step in a proof of it.
- The rule is told to a session directly, with what a session that was not part of the conversation needs, and without the history of how the rule came about.

This replaces the condition of section 16.13 for work outside the frontier (an attempt that finds nothing on the frontier it can take on): the two conditions above are the ones a guide now gives.

**What is in the guides now.** The session that operates the instance read the guides at commit `4f6a6e1b` and reported the following. It is a lead, and the repairer confirms each passage in the copy.

- The guides already state much of the rule. `reference/common.md`, "Working directly on the central difficulty", names the two easier kinds of work a session turns to and says "You should do neither. A problem being hard is not a reason to stop working on it." `roles/attempt.md`, where it describes the choice of work, calls taking a piece "because it is the piece you can see how to close" the retreat from the core that this condition asks a session not to make, and ends: "ask where the root is actually held up, and whether your choice is aimed there."
- While those passages were in force, two attempts of 26 November ended without registering a section, each on the ground that it saw no way forward; one wrote that a section cannot be withdrawn and that it did not want to occupy a statement on which it had no approach, and spent the rest of its time on a bounded computation off the frontier. Of the last 21 attempts that registered a section, 3 had registered on a statement that was on the frontier when the count was made. So text of the same kind, added beside what stands, is unlikely to be enough.
- Five things the rule asks were not found in the guides: weighing the chance of success, the gain and the insight together, with difficulty by itself counting neither for nor against; trying an important obstacle when failure is likely; valuing effort where it matters above a result where it matters less; counting insight and general patterns in their own right; and the two conditions for work beyond the frontier.
- Six passages pull against the rule. (a) `roles/attempt.md` makes ability to contribute part of the choice: "choose, among the work that remains before the root is settled, something you can move forward", and "where you can contribute" among the things to judge. (b) `reference/sections.md`: "A registered section cannot be withdrawn, so register it once you have decided to pursue it", and `roles/attempt.md`: "Your sections fix what counts as done". (c) `roles/attempt.md` ties staying power to a statement: "Stay with the statement you set out to settle and the blocked argument", "Tenacity belongs to settling what you took on"; the rule speaks of staying with the obstacle, and an obstacle can be common to many statements and be no element itself. (d) `roles/attempt.md`: "research that does not serve it stays outside the attempt's purpose", by which readers counted insight only by its bearing on the root. (e) The same passage ties looking beyond the frontier to finding "nothing on the frontier that you can take on". (f) Every session receives the rule, so many may aim at one obstacle, and `roles/attempt.md` tells a session to "leave proving or refuting that part to its holder".

**The structure to reach.** The guides a producing session receives say one thing about how work is chosen, and it is the rule. That is reached by bringing into agreement the passages that pull against it and by saying what the guides lack where the choice is described, and not by adding the rule as a further passage beside them (freeze document, 「가이드를 고치는 수리에 걸리는 것」: one home for a matter, and no second copy for the same reader). The common guide's section states the standing condition for every role; the attempt's guide applies it to the moment of choosing; the text a resumed attempt receives says it at the moment the rest of the pack may be gone. The common guide is also in the pack of a review. What it says of the rule therefore stays a condition on a session's own work, and where a sentence there could be read by a review as a ground for a verdict, a clause says that it is none; that clause comes from the paragraph of the user's text on the standing of the rule, which a guide may draw on. The words "You should follow it, and it is more than advice" need not stand in a guide: a guide carries the force of the rule by giving it as the user's condition with its reason, as the common section does for the condition it states now. Every other detail of the passages that are reworded stays. The guides give a session reasons and leave it the judgment, as the user's own two texts in the pack do.

**What is not decided, and is not settled by the correction.** The session that sent the rule listed five matters the user has not decided: whether an attempt registers a section while it is still looking for a way in, and what registering means then; what a session registers when an obstacle spans many statements; whether many sessions at one obstacle is wanted, tolerated or limited; what a session leaves behind when it ends without a result; and how to tell that the change worked. Passages (b), (c) and (f) above touch the first three. There the correction changes only what contradicts the rule's own words, and leaves what registration means, what a section fixes and what a session does about another's holding as they stand; the sentence of `reference/sections.md` quoted under (b) says nothing of difficulty and is not changed by this item. The repairer raises no proposal for the five. It traces each of them in the guides and the program and writes what they say today into its record, under a heading `### 정하지 않은 다섯`, in a paragraph for each. The user has since left decisions of this kind to the driving session for the rest of the run (section 3, the last subsection), and it settles them from that record before the next round.

**What moves together**, raised by the repairer of the area of coordination, questions and the producing roles (6): the passages of `roles/attempt.md` on choosing what to take on and on holding on to the problem; "Working directly on the central difficulty" in `reference/common.md`; `resume/attempt.md`; `roles/explore.md`, `roles/formalizer.md` and `roles/peer.md` where they speak of choosing work or of turning to easier work, as far as the files show that they do; the sentence of `reference/retained-work.md`, under "Reporting that all the work that remains is occupied", that carries the condition of section 16.13; and `reference/sections.md` only within the limit of the paragraph above. The pieces that state the rule and the pieces that remove what pulls against it are consistent only together and are one item. A passage that can be brought into agreement by itself, leaving the guides consistent whether or not the rest is applied, may be raised as an item of its own, so that one lost detail does not undo the whole. No program is changed. If a test asserts a sentence that is reworded, it moves with it.

Section 16.24 changes other parts of `reference/retained-work.md`, `resume/common.md` and one sentence of `roles/peer.md`; this direction leaves those to it.

**What round 13 applied.** Three items, in guides only. The first changes "Working directly on the central difficulty" in `reference/common.md` (one paragraph reworded, two added) and, in `roles/attempt.md`, the passages on choosing what to take on (three paragraphs reworded, one added); the two are consistent only together. The second brings the text a resumed attempt receives (`resume/attempt.md`) into agreement, and the third the sentence of `roles/explore.md` on choosing a question. The repairer left the sentence of `reference/retained-work.md` under "Reporting that all the work that remains is occupied" as it was, because it does not say when work beyond the frontier is chosen, and left the passages named above under (b), (c) and (f), which touch the matters that were not decided. Its record traces the five matters in the guides and the program. The driving session settled them from that record; section 16.29 gives what it settled and what a guide is to say.

### 16.28 The modules of the root's definitions are locked, and the root check compares their files

A decision of the user (27 November), on a proposal of round 12 about how far the check of the root sees (section 4.4 had recorded that limit as told to the user and not ruled on). Carried out in round 14; the last two paragraphs of this section say what the correction did and what is left. Round 13 had written it out first, and that item was rejected for two sentences (below, "What round 13 wrote, and why it was not applied").

The driving session had put three options. The user asked whether the root's statement and definitions could be frozen "in a file that should not be changed, to be fully complete and self sufficient, with the research only connecting to it", so that "comparing the file hash would hold meaning", was told what exists and how a lock would work, and decided: "lock". That is the second option, comparing files, together with a refusal of a candidate that would change such a file.

What stands behind it. At `init` the operator puts the definitions into modules of their own and the root's statement into a module that imports them (`reference/operations.md`, where the root is fixed in Lean). The finalize command calls the modules that the root's module imported in the first commit, directly or through one another, the definition modules (`definition_modules` in `runtime/package.py`), and requires each to be, byte for byte, the file of the first commit. During research nothing requires that. The check of a candidate (`check_applied` in `runtime/canon.py`, which `build_candidate` in `runtime/canon_tool.py` runs when a peer or an organizer prepares a candidate) refuses a statement that changed in place and a definition that is replaced, and looks at nothing else of a module's file. Two publications can change the file of a definition module: the replacement of a theorem that stands in it, which changes the proof and can add imports to the module's header (`apply_lean`), and an organizer's rearrangement (`apply_rearrangement`). A changed header can make Lean read the unchanged text of a definition as another term; for a statement the check of a candidate catches that, and for the body of a definition nothing does. The check of the root (`Runner.check_root` in `runtime/runner.py`, through `canon.statement_differences`) compares, for each element under the root's statement, the kind and the elaborated statement, and for a definition the text; it reads the two indexes and not the files. So `status` can report the root as unchanged for an instance that the finalize command refuses. In the instance on this machine the definition module is still the file of the first commit.

The decision, in two parts.

- *The lock.* A candidate whose application changes the file of a definition module is refused, with a sentence that names the module. The root's module is not locked: its file changes when the proof of the root enters, and its statement is protected by the refusal that exists. A module that the root's module does not import is not locked either, so a theorem the operator supplied with an open proof outside the definition modules is still proved where it stands.
- *The comparison at the end.* The check of the root also compares the definition modules with the first commit and reports a difference in `differences`, so that an edit made by hand shows as well.

**What is the user's, and what is design.** The user's are these: the files of the definition modules are locked against publications; the definition modules are the modules that the root's module imported when the instance was created, directly or through one another; the root's own module and the modules it does not import are not locked; and the check of the root compares the files of the definition modules with the first commit and reports a difference. A correction does not change these. Everything else is design, of the driving session or of the repairer of round 13, and the repairer settles it from the files: at which point of applying a candidate the refusal stands, with what the applied file is compared and by what means, how the definition modules are found, the wording of the refusal and of the sentence in `differences`, and the tests.

One point of design the driving session settles here, because the two readings lead to different programs. The refusal stands wherever a candidate is applied to the canon: when it is prepared, and again when the runner publishes it. Without the second, a candidate that was prepared before the correction is published unchecked. This was the course of round 13's item, and the repairer may settle it otherwise from the files and say why.

What the user was told it costs. A theorem that stands in a definition module with an open proof can never be proved there; the operator's manual already tells the operator not to put one there, and such a research cannot be packaged today. An organizer cannot rearrange a definition module. At the preparation the lock takes effect as soon as the correction is in the files, since `prepare` runs as a process of its own; what the runner does, at publication and in the check of the root, takes effect with a runner started afterwards.

What is not part of it: a hash of the body term of each definition (the third option), and a root whose statement itself stands in a file that never changes, which would change how a root is posed and settled. A change of Lean or of a pinned library, under which unchanged files can mean something else, stays what `reference/operations.md` says of it.

**What round 13 wrote, and why it was not applied.** The repairer of this round does not open round 13's files; what it has of that item is this account. The item had twenty-seven pieces.

- It added two functions to `runtime/canon.py`. `definition_modules` gives the modules that the root's module imported in the first commit, directly or through one another (the set that `package.definition_modules` uses for a package); it reads the module of the root from the first commit's index and the imports from the headers of the Lean files. `check_definition_modules` compares each module that an application wrote or deleted with the file the canon holds at that moment, byte for byte, and refuses with `CanonError` when a module that differs is a definition module. It asks Git for the definition modules only when the file of some module that the canon already had differs, so a candidate that only adds modules costs nothing more.
- Both places that apply a candidate called it right after the application and before any build: `build_candidate` in `runtime/canon_tool.py`, and `Runner.advance_build` in `runtime/runner.py`. Both already pass a `CanonError` of the application on, to the errors of `prepare` and to the reason of a failed publication, so no new path was needed.
- The sentence of the refusal named the module, said that the modules the root's module imported when the instance was created hold the definitions the root was posed with and that their files do not change, and said what to do instead: a new proof of a statement that stands in such a module enters as a separate declaration in a new module, and a rearrangement leaves such a module as it is.
- `Runner.check_root` asked Git which of the definition modules differ from the first commit (`git diff --name-only --no-renames -z <first commit> -- <paths>`) and added to `differences` a sentence for each, of the form "the file of the definition module <module> is not the file the first commit has".
- Tests through the real path, with a variant of the tests' canon in which the root's module reaches the definitions through a second module and a further module is not imported by the root's module at all.
- Four paragraphs of `reference/operations.md`, and one entry each in the lists of `reference/change-format.md` and `reference/organizer-output.md` that say what the preparation checks.

It departed from what this section then proposed in three places and gave its reasons. The refusal stood right after the application and not inside `check_applied`, which receives two indexes and no files and runs after the build. The applied file was compared with the file the canon holds and not with the first commit's, which is the same comparison for an instance whose definition modules never changed, and is not exposed to line endings. And the runner made the same check at publication. One judge rejected the item for two sentences, and since an item needs all three approvals and is applied whole, that rejected all of it:
