## 22. The compaction of a session's context: what was measured, what was tried, and what the user decided

This section was added between round 10 and round 11. It is a record in the sense of section 1. It gives the substance of two reports, `임시/do-math_검증_20261125-3/조사/E1_압축과_수학.md` (an investigation of what real sessions kept and lost) and `임시/do-math_검증_20261125-3/조사/E2_메시지_훅_시험.md` (a trial of a way to reach a session inside a turn), and says which of the possible changes the user chose. The directions that follow from the user's choices are sections 16.17 and 16.24; the user's statements are in section 3.

### What a compaction is here

A worker session is launched with a context window of one million tokens. When its context nears the compaction window set in `runtime/launch.py`, the client replaces the conversation with a summary and the session goes on from there. The investigation read the retained streams of the instance running on this machine on 26 November: 206 compactions in 203 sessions, 101 of them in peers, 56 in attempts and 49 in explorers, none in a review, a formalizer or an organizer. All were in sessions of the Claude CLI; no session of this instance has run on Codex. With the window at 900,000, as it then was, they came at 865,220 to 994,392 tokens, at a median of about 872,000; the step that is under way when the threshold is crossed lands on top of it. Seventy-two fell in a session's first turn, in the middle of its work or as it began to write up. The other 134 fell in the resumed turn, a few tool calls after the host had resumed a session whose first turn had ended at about 790,000 tokens with its report written.

### What is in the context, and what replaces it

More than half of what the context holds at that moment is the session's own reasoning: a median 58 percent of the tokens in attempts, 53 in explorers and 32 in peers. That reasoning is kept nowhere in readable form. The stream shows the thinking blocks empty, and the transcript the client keeps holds them in a form nobody can read. What a session wrote in visible messages before a compaction is a few thousand characters.

Three things replace the conversation. The first is a summary that the session's own model writes in one response, 18,543 to 34,294 characters long, in nine fixed sections made for programming work; the two sections that carry mathematics are about a fifth of it. The request that produces it carries the session's system prompt. The second is the last step of the conversation, kept as it was. The third is up to five files that the session most recently read or edited with the file tools, with their content only where a file is under about 5,000 tokens; a session's report came back as a bare path in 75 of 104 compactions of attempts and explorers. The summary ends by sending the session to its transcript for "specific details from before compaction", and the transcript does not hold the reasoning.

The summary keeps statements with their status, numbers, explicit counterexamples and the names of files well. It keeps a proof whole where the session judges that proof current and not yet written: 106 of the 206 summaries say in words that something is in no file and go on to state it. It reduces what lay further back to a clause; in one session a lemma survived only as its name, in another four failed attempts at a proof as a parenthesis. In the three sessions that were compacted twice, the second summary dropped most of the names the first had carried: what crosses two compactions is what reached the files in between.

### What was lost, and what decides it

Of nineteen sessions read closely, four went looking for their own earlier reasoning after a compaction (the statement of a lemma, the details of failed proofs, an argument, a remark), searched the transcript and found nothing. Two derived what they needed again; in two the item is absent from the final report. In fourteen no loss of mathematics was found, and no result was contradicted or proved again in another form. Over all 206 compactions, 57 were followed by some sign of recovering something. A loss the session itself did not notice leaves no trace, so these are lower bounds.

What separated the sessions was whether the mathematics stood in the session's own files when the compaction came. In the resumed turn it does, and sessions there read their report and went on. In the first turn, 11 of 49 compactions of attempts and explorers found no report text at all. What a session did first afterwards, and how many minutes had passed since it last wrote, separated nothing.

Before round 10 no guide a session receives said that its conversation is replaced during its work, that its reasoning is stored nowhere, or what comes back. The guides asked a producing session to start its report early and keep it current, and gave as the reason an interruption from outside and the peer who takes over.

### What round 10 changed

Round 10 worked from the user's first statement only ("focus on better survival of a compaction"), before the decisions below were made, and under the rule that a guide gives a session knowledge and sets it no rhythm. It changed three things. `reference/retained-work.md` has a section, "What a compaction of your context leaves", which tells a session that its conversation is replaced by a summary, what comes through and what does not, and that the CLI's record of the conversation does not hold its reasoning. `resume/common.md` says in one sentence that after a compaction a session's files are what remains exact. And `conversation.md`, the rendering of a producer's conversation that a peer reads, no longer spells out the counters and identifiers of every response of the API, which had been 13 to 18 percent of that file in three sessions. Round 11 wrote out the direction of section 16.17 and the item was rejected for one test. Round 12 applied it: a worker that receives messages runs one hook, the host sends one message at 800,000 tokens, and the operator has a command that sends a text to a running session. The compaction window and the way a session ends were not changed by it. Round 13 changed them, by section 16.24: a session whose task is not done answers the message by handing the task over to a new session of its role, and a Claude worker that receives messages is launched with automatic compaction off. The section of `reference/retained-work.md` named above is now "Handing a task over to a new session"; it keeps the facts of a compaction for a session whose conversation is still replaced by a summary.

### What the user decided

The investigation named the means the two clients offer and the driving session put them to the user with what each costs. The user's decisions, in the order they were made (section 3 has the words):

| Matter | Decision |
|---|---|
| The facts of a compaction in the worker's system prompt, which survives a compaction and is sent with the request that writes the summary | yes, and withdrawn the same day (end of section 16) |
| Telling a session to write a finished part of its work into its files when it is finished, before the whole task is done | yes, and withdrawn the same day (end of section 16) |
| A hook that gives the summary its instructions; a hook that puts material back after a compaction; summaries of thinking kept in the transcript; a command that shows a session how full its context is | not chosen |
| A way to send a message into a running session, used by the host to tell a session at 800,000 tokens to wrap up before the compaction, and open to the operator for any message | yes (section 16.17; applied in round 12) |
| The hooks of the operator's project and account | kept out of workers (section 16.17; applied in round 12) |
| The compaction window | 950,000 tokens, at which the client compacts at about 916,000; changed by the driving session before round 11. Since round 13 it holds only for a session that cannot be sent the message (next row) |
| After round 11: a session whose task is not done at 800,000 tokens hands it over to a new session of its role, which continues from the report; automatic compaction is switched off | yes (section 16.24; applied in round 13) |

The threshold of the message, and that automatic compaction is off, are the user's choices. A correction does not change them, and a proposal to change them is not raised.

### What the trials showed

A message can reach a session inside a turn only through something the client runs. The trial started three short real sessions with a worker's flags and one hook in the worker's own settings file, and its record says what it did and did not establish. In short: such a hook runs while a hook of the project's settings stays out; text it returns after a batch of tool calls reaches the session with the results of that batch, once; inside a sub-agent the hook's input names the sub-agent, so a hook can hand a message to the session and not to a sub-agent; and the stream the host records shows nothing of the delivery. No session with a worker's own model has yet received a message, and what a research session does with one will be seen only when real sessions run with it.

A second trial, while round 11 was being judged, used the hook program that round's repairer had written, on three more real sessions. Given as a program with its arguments, the hook handed a waiting message over once. A hook that ended with exit code 1 left the session undisturbed. And with the hook's program file missing, the interpreter ended with exit code 2, which the client takes at this event as an order to stop before the next request to the model: the turn ended after its first tool call and the client reported a success. Section 16.17 says what that asks of the correction.

A third trial, after the user had decided to switch automatic compaction off, showed what happens at the limit of the window without it: the request that would exceed the window is refused, the turn ends with exit code 1, and the `result` event names the ground (`terminal_reason` `blocking_limit`). Section 16.24 gives the details. A fourth trial, while round 12 was being judged, used the hook command of that round's correction on two real sessions: with the hook program present a waiting message arrived once, and with it missing the command ended with exit code 1 and the session went on undisturbed. A trial of how a real session takes the wording of the message at 800,000 tokens was planned and was not run.
